And it came to pass, etc. Ὕδωρ , water, whence the dropsy, a watery disease beneath the skin, born of a defect of the bladder, with swelling distension and fetid breath. And it is proper to the man with the dropsy that the more he drinks, the more he thirsts; and therefore he is compared to the man whom the overflowing flux of carnal pleasures weighs down, and he is compared also to the covetous rich man.

VERS. 3.-- Is it lawful, etc. On the sabbaths especially Jesus teaches and works, not only because of the spiritual sabbath, but also because of the more crowded gathering of the people, 306Dseeking the salvation of all.

VERS. 4.-- But they held their peace. Rightly do they hold their peace, who see that whatever he had said would be said against them. For if it is lawful, why do they watch him? If it is not lawful, why do they care for their cattle? But he. He therefore heals the man with the dropsy before the Pharisees, and straightway disputes against avarice, that he may convict them of the avarice through which they violate the sabbath and ill interpret the law. For on the sabbath one must keep holiday not from good but from evil works, whence it is said: No servile work shall you do therein (Lev. XXIII), that is, sin. So in the eternal rest there shall be holiday from evils only, not from good things.

VERS. 5.-- Which of you. By a fitting example he solves the question, that he may show that they violate the sabbath 307Ain a work of covetousness who accuse him of violating it in a work of charity. An ox into a pit. Fittingly does he compare the man with the dropsy to an animal that has fallen into a pit, because he was perishing by a harmful humour. And well does he name the ox and the ass, that he may signify either the wise or the dull, or both peoples, namely the Jewish, pressed by the yoke of the law, and the Gentile, tamed by no reason but stolid and brutish; all of whom, sunk in the pit of concupiscence, he draws out: For all have sinned and do need the grace of God (Rom. XXXV). [Vulg. Rom 3:23 reads egent gloria Dei, "do need the glory of God."]

VERS. 7.-- And he said. After he had convicted them of avarice and of the violation of the sabbath, he begins to teach concerning the grace of the Gospel those present who were fit, inviting them to humility against the swelling which the Pharisees had in the law, or intending in his teaching 307Bhow they ought to choose the places at table in the Church.

VERS. 8.-- When thou art invited: When, called through the grace of faith by a preacher, thou hast joined thyself to the members of the Church, do not, by glorying in thy merits, exalt thyself as though loftier than the rest. Lest perhaps one more honourable. He gives place to one more honourable invited after him, who, made too secure by confidence in his own manner of life, is quickly outstripped by the nimbleness of those who followed him in Christ. And he holds the last place with blushing, etc., when, coming to know better things of others, he humbles whatever lofty thing he had thought of his own working, saying with the Prophet: But being exalted, I have been humbled and have confessed (Psal. LXXXVII). [Vulg. Ps 87:16 reads conturbatus sum, "I have been troubled"; the plate prints confessus, read at 450 dpi on leaf n159.]

VERS. 9.-- And then thou begin, etc. And then, blushing that thou didst once presume greater things of thyself, thou shalt begin to be humbled unto repentance, and to know thyself 307Cof lesser merit, lest thou now begin to seek what is kept for thee at the end, because the inheritance to which men hasten in the beginning shall be wanting in the last blessing.

VERS. 10.-- Then shalt thou have glory, etc. Daily the Lord, while he visits and judges his guests, bestows such gifts of his Spirit upon the humble that the company of the faithful glorifies them with wonder; and, astonished, it leaps forth into the praise of its author, saying: But to me thy friends, O God, are made exceedingly honourable (Psal. CXXXVIII).

VERS. 12.-- And he said. According to the order of the letter. After he had admonished those invited how they ought to sit at table, he admonishes the inviter also whom he ought to invite, lest the temporal spending of goods which he makes 307Dpass away in vain, or perhaps increase his sin: but let him so spend temporal things that through them a way may lie open to things eternal. And to him that had invited him. He speaks to the chief of the Pharisees, who perhaps might grow proud because he had called so many to the banquet as though out of mercy; or, as may be gathered from the Lord's words, he had rather invited them for this, that he might be invited by them in turn, and so no special recompense would follow. Do not call thy friends. That brethren and friends and the rich should hold banquets for one another he does not forbid as though it were a crime, but he shows that it does not avail for the rewards of life. And there are certain banquets of such rich men which receive not only requitals in the present but even damnation in the time to come; whence the Apostle: Not in rioting 308Aand drunkenness (Rom. VIII), that is, in luxurious banquets, which are either celebrated by the contribution of all or are wont to be furnished by turns among companions, where no man is ashamed to do or say anything unseemly. Lest perhaps. As if he said: If thou purposest to invite that thou mayest be invited, therein thou canst be cheated; but if thou distribute to the poor, thou shalt not be cheated of thy reward.

VERS. 13.-- Call the poor. He that calls the poor shall receive a reward in the time to come. He that calls friends and brethren and the rich has received his reward. But if he does this for God's sake, after the example of the sons of Job, then, as in the other offices of brotherly love, he himself who commanded it rewards it. He that calls the wanton for the sake of lasciviousness shall not lack punishment.

VERS. 14.-- In the resurrection. Though all are to rise again, 308Byet the resurrection of the just is called singular and as it were their own, since they are to be changed in it.

VERS. 15.-- When one had heard these things, etc. Allured by the sweetness of the resurrection he had heard of, he breaks forth into the praise of it, and asserts that men shall be truly blessed therein, although he does not well understand by what merits that blessedness may be attained. Blessed, etc. If any man eat of this bread, he shall live for ever (Joan. VI). If any man, perfectly incorporated into the sacrament of the incarnation, shall have deserved to enjoy the vision of the divine majesty, he shall rejoice in eternal blessedness. But because many receive it only so far as faith, as it were by praying, yet loathe to attain its sweetness truly, their torpor is reproved.

VERS. 16.-- A certain man, etc. The Word incarnate 308Chas prepared for many the fulness of inward sweetness, but few come, because many who are subject to him through faith gainsay him continually by living ill. A great supper. This banquet is called not a dinner but a supper, because after the dinner of which Matthew treats a supper remains, and after the supper no banquet remains: from the dinner many are cast out, and from the supper no one.

VERS. 17.-- And he sent his servant. The hour of the supper is the end of the world; whence the Apostle: We know upon whom the ends of the ages are come (I Cor. X). In this end the servant is sent, that is, the order of preachers, to those invited through the law and the prophets, that, loathing being put away, they might prepare themselves to taste the supper, because all things are now ready. For Christ being sacrificed, the entrance of the kingdom 308Dlies open.

VERS. 18.-- And they began, etc. They therefore make excuse, because the kingdom is shut against no man save him whom the profession of his own voice shall have shut out. And every man excuses himself who loves earthly things more than heavenly, even if he say that he is reaching toward heavenly things. I have bought a farm. The farm is earthly substance. He goes out therefore to see the farm who thinks of outward things alone for the sake of substance; and this is what is first sought by the lovers of the world, namely the substance of the body.

(AUG.) In the farm that is bought, lordship and pride are noted. For to have a farm, etc., as far as the love of earthly things is birdlime to spiritual wings.

309AI pray thee. While he says: I pray thee, hold me excused, humility sounds in his voice while he scorns to come, pride in his deed.

VERS. 19.-- Yokes of oxen. The five yoke of oxen are the five senses of the body, which are rightly called yokes, since they are doubled in either sex; and because they know and touch not inward things but outward only, by them curiosity is rightly designated, which, while it searches out the life of a neighbour without, knows not its own inward parts. And the mind of a curious man, the more skilled it is in what is another's, the more ignorant it is of its own. Nor do the words of the man excusing himself disagree with the signification of his own vice, when he says: I go to try them. For trying is wont to belong to curiosity.

VERS. 20.-- I have married a wife. Because many take wives not for the sake of fruitfulness but for the desires of the flesh, 309Btherefore by this thing the pleasure of the flesh is designated, for the sake of which the fastidious man refuses to come to the supper of God.

VERS. 22.-- It is done, etc. Many such of the Jews have we gathered to thy supper, but these do not fill the place of thy banquet; there remains a place where the great number of the Gentiles may be received.

VERS. 23.-- Go out into the highways and hedges, etc. Note that in this third invitation it is not said, Invite, but Compel them to come in. For they who, broken by the adversities of this world, return to the love of God, and are corrected from the desires of the present life, do not enter unless compelled. But some are called and despise it, who receive the house of understanding but do not fulfil it in work. Some are called and come, who fulfil in work the 309Cunderstanding they have received.

VERS. 25.-- And there went crowds, etc. The crowds that follow Jesus as he makes his way to Jerusalem signify those who, invited or compelled, come to the supper, and with their whole intent imitate Jesus going before them to the vision of eternal peace. These therefore he instructs, who ought with sincere love to seek that country, and for its sake to renounce the affections of carnal love.

VERS. 26.-- If any man come. We can at once both hate and love: so that we love both those joined to us by acquaintance and by the divine precept, and those whom we suffer as adversaries in the way of God we may not know, by hating and fleeing them. Yea more. Then do we rightly hate our own soul, when we do not consent to its carnal desires, but struggle against them and break them.

309DVERS. 27.-- And whosoever doth not carry. The cross is carried in two ways: either when the flesh is afflicted by abstinence, or when the mind is afflicted by compassion for a neighbour. And come after me. For if a man tears his flesh for empty glory, or has compassion on his neighbour carnally so as to favour his fault, he seems to bear the cross, but he does not follow God.

VERS. 28.-- For which of you. After the Lord had given to those following him into Jerusalem lofty precepts concerning contempt of the world and the casting away of the body, he adds a precept concerning perseverance, for which patience with humility is necessary. And this by the likeness of one that builds. To build a tower. Because he had given lofty precepts, he subjoins a comparison of a loftiness to be built. 310AReckoneth the charges. He reckons the charges who discerns that temporal money must be paid out, and the heart turned away from the covetous desires of the world, and the soul made ready against the adversities of the world.

VERS. 29.-- All that see. As the Apostle says: We are made a spectacle to the world, to angels and to men (II Cor. IV). And in everything that we do we ought to consider our hidden adversaries, who lie in wait for our works; and unless we watch against them, we suffer as mockers those whom we have as persuaders to evil.

VERS. 31.-- Or what, etc. After the similitude of the tower to be built, he adds the similitude of a greater matter, of a king about to fight. In which it is shown that by the fulfilment of the preceding commandments no man can attain to glory 310Bunless the sentence of the severe judge be tempered by mercy. For in the sight of God every man is imperfect. If he be able. He meets God as it were with ten thousand who offers the works of the ten commandments which he has performed outwardly. But God comes against him as it were with a doubled number, when he examines him, scarcely prepared in work alone, at once concerning both work and thought. And therefore, while he is still far off through the delay of the judgment, we, considering the insufficiency of our frailty, ought to send before us, for the obtaining of the severe judge's peace, an embassy of tears, of good works, of holy affection.

VERS. 33.-- So likewise, etc. He infers this from the foregoing similitudes. Since he cannot build well who does not reckon the charges, nor can he safely go to meet another 310Cwho does not send an embassy before him, therefore neither can he be my disciple who does not renounce all things.

VERS. 34.-- Good. Truly, whoever wishes to be my disciple must renounce all things, for so he will be good salt, that is, worthy to be set in the number of the apostles, for the seasoning of the hearts of his hearers with spiritual wisdom.

VERS. 35.-- Nor into the dunghill. Salt makes the ground barren. Whence the wrath of conquerors used to sow with salt the cities they had destroyed, that no shoot might spring up in them. So the apostles, the kingdom of sin being destroyed, restrain the shoot of vices. Salt seasons food and dries flesh. Preaching keeps human nature unharmed for its Creator from the worms and the rottenness of vices.

CAPUT XV.

  • 310D

VERS. 4.-- Qui ex vobis, etc. Didiceras in superioribus abnegare negligentiam, vitare arrogantiam, devotionem sumere, saecularibus occupationibus non teneri, caduca non praeferre perpetuis. Sed quia fragilitas humana firmum nequit in tanto saeculi lubrico tenere vestigium, etiam adversus errorem remedium ibi medicus demonstrat, scilicet, judex spem veniae non negat. Centum oves. Quia centenarius numerus perfectus est. Deus centum oves, id est perfectum numerum habuit, cum angelorum et hominum substantiam creavit; sed una periit, quando homo pascua vitae peccando dereliquit: ergo quia 311Arationalis creaturae numerus pereunte homine erat diminutus, quaerit in terra hominem ut summa integretur.

Nonne dimittit. Quasi dicat: Murmuratis, quia peccatores recipio, sed ego non veni nisi ut aberrantes colligam, sicut unusquisque vestrum aberrantem ovem reducere laborat.

VERS. 5.-- Imponit in humerum. Ovem in humeris posuit, qui, humanam naturam suscipiens, peccata nostra ipse portavit: unde humeri Christi crucis brachia sunt, illic peccata nostra deposuit, in illa nobilis patibuli cervice requievit.

VERS. 6.-- Et veniens. Reparato homine ad coelum redit pastor, ibi angelorum choros invenit. Qui amici, quia in stabilitate sua voluntatem ejus continue 311Bcustodiunt; qui et vicini, quia claritate visionis ejus fruuntur. Et nota, quod non dicit: Congratulamini ovi inventae, sed mihi, quia gaudium ejus est vita nostra.

VERS. 7.-- Dico vobis. In nonaginta novem, justorum numerus intelligitur; in una ove, peccatorum. Gaudium. Angeli, quia rationales, gaudent de reconciliato sibi homine, quod incendit nos ad probitatem: agamus quod illis gratum sit, quorum et affectare patrocinium, et offensam timere debemus. Quam super. Si in nonaginta novem justis qui non erraverunt nec indigent poenitentia, angeli intelliguntur, majus videtur impios justificare quam justos creare et conservare, quia etsi utrumque aequalis potentiae, hic majoris misericordiae et majoris gaudii.

311CVERS. 8.-- Aut quae mulier. Mulier, id est Dei sapientia, decem drachmas habuit, cum homines et angelos ad imaginem suam creavit. Sed unam perdidit, cum homo a similitudine Creatoris recessit. Sed Dei sapientia lucernam ad quaerendum accendit, cum in carne apparuit, quia lucerna est lux in testa, id est verbum in carne. Quod ubi inter homines claruit, domum evertit, quia conscientias hominum de reatu suo perturbavit, et sic drachma reperitur, dum in homine similitudo Conditoris reparatur. Habens drachmas decem. Angelorum et hominum naturam, quam ad aeternitatem Dominus consistere voluit, ad suam similitudinem creavit. Decem ergo drachmas mulier habuit, cum novem ordinibus angelorum additus est homo, ut compleretur decimus 311Delectorum numerus. Qui nec post culpam a Creatore periit, quia eum sapientia Dei per carnem miraculis coruscans, ex lumine testae reparavit. Convocat. Amicae et vicinae supernae potestates, quae tanto Deo juxta sunt, quanto eum vicinius contemplantur. Quas vocat, dum per exhibitionem gratiae hominibus impensae ad amorem sui vehementius accendit.

VERS. 11.-- Homo quidam. Duabus praemissis parabolis, quantum ipse cum angelis gaudeat de poenitentium salute insinuat. In hac tertia non solum gaudium ostendit, sed etiam murmur invidentium reprehendit.

VERS. 12.-- Et dixit, etc. Sicut Deus non habet alium a quo formetur, a quo negatur, sic iste vult 312Asua potestate uti, ut nullo formante, nullo regente vivat. Pater, da. Dimidium patrimonium petentibus datur, nec est culpa patris qui dat adolescentiori. Nulla in regno Dei infirma aetas, nec fides gravatur annis, nisi ipse certe se judicaverit inidoneum qui poscit. Si iste non recessisset a patre, impedimentum nescisset aetatis, sed peraegre profectus merito patrimonium vastat. Et divisit. Fidelibus suae gratiae protectionem, quam desiderabant, impertiendo: infidelibus vero naturalibus solum ingenii quo contenti erant, beneficium concedendo.

VERS. 13.-- Non post multos. Non multo post institutionem humani generis, placuit animae liberum arbitrium ferre secum, quasi quamdam potentiam naturae suae, et deserere eum a quo condita est, 312Bconfidens viribus suis. Et has vires tanto citius consumat quanto datorem deserit. Haec est prodiga vita, quae amat fundere et spatiari in pompis exterioribus, relinquens eum qui sibi est interior

VERS. 14.-- Et ipse coepit. Quia nihil satis est prodigae voluptati, quia voluptas semper habet famem sui; merito ergo eget, qui thesauros sapientiae, divitiarumque coelestium altitudinem reliquit.

VERS. 15.-- Uni civium. Unus civium ille est qui concupiscentiis terrenis merito pravitatis suae praepositus princeps mundi vocatur. Qui in villam mittit, quia cupiditati mundialis substantiae subjugat. Porcos pascit, cum ea quibus immundi spiritus gaudent, operatur. Ut pasceret porcos. Porcus immundum animal, quod sordibus delectatur; sic diabolus, 312Cqui in idolis cruore pecudum pascitur, et morte ipsius hominis. Misit ergo in villam, id est suum fecit famulum, ut pasceret porcos, immolans ei animam suam.

VERS. 16.-- Et cupiebat. Cibus daemonum est ebrietas, fornicatio et hujusmodi, quae blanda sunt et ad sui usum provocant. Neque alia cura est luxuriosis nisi ut ventrem suum impleant, quorum deus venter est (Philip. III). Sed eis non potest saturari, quia voluptas semper habet famem sui. Nemo illi dabat. Quia diabolus cum aliquem suum facit, ultra ad vitiorum abundantiam non procurat, sciens esse jam mortuum.

VERS. 17.-- Quanti mercenarii. Qui futurae mercedis intuitu digna operari satagunt, quotidianis 312Dsupernae gratiae reficiuntur alimentis. Sed fame perit, qui extra domum patris, id est sine fide vivens, vitam beatam in inani philosophia inquirit.

VERS. 18.-- Pater, peccavi. Etsi Deus omnia novit, vocem tamen exspectat confessionis. Sine periculo prodas quod scis jam esse cognitum: habes causam ut pro te interveniat Christus, nec pro te gratis mortuus sit; habet et causam ignoscendi Pater, quia quod vult Filius vult et Pater.

VERS. 19.-- Fac me, etc. Ad Filii affectum, qui omnia quae Patris sunt sua esse non ambigit, aspirare non praesumit, sed mercenarii statum jam pro mercede serviturus desiderat, sed nec hunc etiam nisi paterna dignatione se promereri posse testatur.

313A(AMBR.) Distat inter servum, mercenarium et filium. Servus, etc., usque ad quod post osculum patris generosissime dedignatur.

VERS. 20.-- Cum autem adhuc longe esset, etc. Antequam intelligeret Deum, sed tamen cum jam pie eum quaereret, tamen nondum dignus operibus rediret, per Verbum incarnatum anticipat filii reditum. Ante ad terras venit quam ille ad domum confessionis, ipsum verbum non deserens, sed in verbo usque ad peregrinationem nostram condescendens. Brachium, id est filium in amplexum revertentis humiliavit. Pater super filii collum cecidit, dum leve jugum suae dilectionis sibi imposuit, et osculatus est eum, ut ostenderetur quod alibi dicitur: Osculetur me osculo oris sui (Cant. I). Rediens, 313Bosculum charitatis a patre accipit, dum per gratiam certificatur de indulgentia.

VERS. 22.-- Cito proferte, etc. Stola prima, vestis innocentiae, in qua primus homo conditus, sed post culpam se nudum novit, et pellicium, id est mortale sumpsit indumentum. Annulum. Id est signaculum fidei, quo signantur promissa in cordibus credentium. In manu. Id est operatione: ut per opera fides clarescat, et per fidem opera firmentur. Et calceamenta. Id est exempla priorum munite in officio praedicandi, ut et opus ornetur bene vivendo, et cursus ad aeterna praeparando.

VERS. 23.-- Adducite vitulum. Praedicate Christum et mortem ejus insinuate, ut et corde credat, 313Coccisum imitando, et ore percipiat passionis sacramentum ad emendationem.

VERS. 24.-- Et coeperunt epulari. Non solum filius pro quo vitulus occisus est, reficitur, sed et pater et servi ejus, quia refectio Dei et sanctorum est salus peccatorum. Et nota, quod post datam stolam, annulum, calceamentum, vitulus immolatur, quia nisi quis spem primae immortalitatis induerit, nisi annulo fidei opera praemunierit, nisi ipsam fidem confitendo praedicaverit, coelestibus non potest interesse sacramentis.

VERS. 25.-- Erat autem filius, etc. Filius, id est populus Israel, qui nec ad colenda idola elongaverat. Neque in domo erat, quia interiora legis non penetrabat: litterali sensu, contentus, terrena operans, 313Dterrena exspectans. Hic est qui agrum emit, et quinque juga boum, legis onere pressus, terrenis sensibus fruitur, et uxorem duxit in affectu carnis. Et cum veniret. Appropinquat filius domum cum aliquis in populo illo spiritualiter legem intelligens, labores servilis operis improbat, et Ecclesiae libertatem considerat. Audit symphoniam et chorum, id est spiritu plenos vocibus consonis Evangelium praedicare. Vocat unum de servis, cum sumit ad legendum aliquem de prophetis. Et eum interrogat, dum in eo scrutatur quae sint haec festa Ecclesiae in quibus se esse non videt.

VERS. 26.-- Interrogavit. Et nunc quaerit Israel cur Deus laetetur in assumptione gentium, et propter 314Ainvidiam nescit patris voluntatem, et foris stat, quamvis per praecones rogetur.

VERS. 28.-- Indignatus. Indignabantur Judaei et conquerebantur, quia Jesus cum gentibus epularetur. Et adhuc de gentium salute indignantur nec volunt introire. Sed cum plenitudo gentium introierit, egredietur pater ad rogandum Israel, quando manifesta erit vocatio Judaeorum.

VERS. 29.-- Ecce tot annis. (HIER.) Justitiam legis secutus, justitiae Dei non est subjectus. Quae enim major est justitia Dei quam ignoscere poenitentibus?

Mandatum tuum, etc. Quasi non sit praeterire, saluti alterius invidere, vel de justitia se jactare.

VERS. 30.-- Occidisti illi vitulum. Confitentur venisse 314BChristum, sed invidia non vult salvari. Sic de Judaeo et gentili. Generaliter autem de justo et peccatore potest accipi. Devoravit substantiam, etc. Meretrices sunt superstitiones gentilium, cum quibus substantiam dissipat, qui, relicto connubio verbi Dei, cum daemone feda cupiditate fornicatur. Filii, tu semper, etc. Non quasi mentientem redarguit, sed secum perseverantiam ejus approbans, et ad perfruitionem potioris exsultationis invitans. Omnia mea, etc. In aeterna beatitudine, et omnium singula, et singulorum omnia.

CHAPTER XV.

  • 310D

VERS. 4.-- Who of you, etc. In what went before thou hadst learned to renounce negligence, to shun arrogance, to take up devotion, not to be held fast by worldly occupations, not to prefer things that fall to things that abide. But because human frailty cannot keep a firm footing on so slippery a world, the physician there shows a remedy against error also, namely, that the judge does not deny the hope of pardon. A hundred sheep. Because the number of a hundred is a perfect number. God had a hundred sheep, that is, a perfect number, when he created the substance of angels and of men; but one perished, when man by sinning forsook the pastures of life: therefore, because 311Athe number of the rational creature was diminished by the perishing of man, he seeks man on earth that the sum may be made whole.

Doth he not leave. As if he said: You murmur, because I receive sinners, but I came not save to gather those going astray, as each one of you labours to bring back a straying sheep.

VERS. 5.-- He layeth it upon his shoulder. He set the sheep upon his shoulders, who, taking up human nature, himself bore our sins: whence the shoulders of Christ are the arms of the cross; there he laid down our sins, on that neck of the noble gibbet he rested.

VERS. 6.-- And coming. When man was repaired, the shepherd returns to heaven; there he finds the choirs of angels. They are friends, because in their steadfastness they keep his will continually; 311Band they are neighbours, because they enjoy the brightness of his vision. And note that he does not say: Rejoice with the sheep that is found, but with me, because his joy is our life.

VERS. 7.-- I say to you. In the ninety-nine, the number of the just is understood; in the one sheep, that of sinners. Joy. The angels, because they are rational, rejoice over a man reconciled to them, which sets us on fire toward uprightness: let us do what is pleasing to them, whose patronage we ought both to court and whose offence we ought to fear. Than over. If in the ninety-nine just who have not erred nor need penitence the angels are understood, it seems a greater thing to justify the impious than to create and preserve the just, because although each is of equal power, here there is greater mercy and greater joy.

311CVERS. 8.-- Or what woman. The woman, that is, the Wisdom of God, had ten drachmas, when she created men and angels to her own image. But she lost one, when man withdrew from the likeness of his Creator. But the Wisdom of God kindled a lamp to seek with, when she appeared in the flesh, because a lamp is a light in an earthen vessel, that is, the Word in flesh. And when this shone forth among men, it overturned the house, because it troubled the consciences of men concerning their own guilt, and so the drachma is found again, while in man the likeness of the Maker is repaired. Having ten drachmas. The nature of angels and of men, which the Lord willed to subsist unto eternity, he created to his own likeness. Ten drachmas therefore the woman had, when to the nine orders of angels man was added, that the tenth 311Dnumber of the elect might be completed. And that number did not perish from its Creator even after the fault, because the Wisdom of God, flashing with miracles through the flesh, repaired it from the light of the earthen vessel. She calleth together. The friends and the neighbours are the powers above, which are near to God in the measure that they contemplate him more nearly. And she calls them, while through the showing of the grace bestowed upon men she kindles them more vehemently to the love of herself.

VERS. 11.-- A certain man. By the two parables set before, he intimates how greatly he himself with the angels rejoices over the salvation of the penitent. In this third one he not only shows the joy, but also rebukes the murmur of the envious.

VERS. 12.-- And he said, etc. As God has no other by whom he may be formed, by whom he is denied, so this man wishes to use his own power, 312Athat he may live with none forming him, none ruling him. Father, give. The half of the patrimony is given to those who ask, nor is it the fault of the father who gives to the younger. No age is weak in the kingdom of God, nor is faith weighed down by years, unless indeed he who asks has judged himself unfit. If this man had not withdrawn from his father, he would have known no hindrance of age, but having journeyed into a far country he deservedly wastes the patrimony. And he divided. To the faithful, by imparting the protection of his grace which they desired: but to the unfaithful, by granting only the natural benefit of native wit, with which they were content.

VERS. 13.-- Not after many. Not long after the institution of the human race, it pleased the soul to carry off with it free choice, as a kind of power of its own nature, and to desert him by whom it was founded, 312Btrusting in its own strength. And it consumes those strengths the more swiftly, the more it deserts the giver. This is the prodigal life, which loves to pour itself out and to range abroad in outward shows, leaving him who is more inward to it

VERS. 14.-- And he began. Because nothing is enough for prodigal pleasure, because pleasure always has a hunger of itself; deservedly therefore is he in want who has left behind the treasures of wisdom and the height of heavenly riches.

VERS. 15.-- To one of the citizens. That one of the citizens is he who, being set over earthly concupiscences by the desert of his own depravity, is called the prince of the world. Who sends him into a farm, because he subjects him to the greed of worldly substance. He feeds swine, when he works those things in which unclean spirits rejoice. That he might feed swine. The swine is an unclean animal, which delights in filth; so the devil, 312Cwho is fed in idols with the blood of cattle, and with the death of man himself. He sent him therefore into the farm, that is, made him his own servant, that he might feed swine, sacrificing to him his own soul.

VERS. 16.-- And he desired. The food of demons is drunkenness, fornication and the like, which are enticing and provoke to their own use. Nor have the wanton any other care save to fill their belly, whose god is their belly (Philip. III). But he cannot be satisfied with them, because pleasure always has a hunger of itself. No man gave unto him. Because when the devil makes any man his own, he takes no further trouble for an abundance of vices, knowing him to be already dead.

VERS. 17.-- How many hired servants. They who with an eye to the reward to come are busy to work worthy things, are refreshed with the daily 312Dnourishment of grace from above. But he perishes of hunger who, living outside the father's house, that is, without faith, seeks the blessed life in empty philosophy.

VERS. 18.-- Father, I have sinned. Although God knows all things, yet he awaits the voice of confession. Without danger thou mayest disclose what thou knowest is already known: thou hast a cause why Christ should intercede for thee, nor has he died for thee in vain; the Father too has a cause for pardoning, because what the Son wills the Father also wills.

VERS. 19.-- Make me, etc. He does not presume to aspire to the affection of a son, who does not doubt that all that is the Father's is his own, but he desires the station of a hired servant, now about to serve for wages, and yet he testifies that he cannot earn even this except by the father's condescension.

313A(AMBR.) There is a difference between a slave, a hired servant and a son. A slave, etc., as far as which after the father's kiss he most nobly disdains.

VERS. 20.-- But when he was yet a great way off, etc. Before he understood God, but yet when he was already seeking him dutifully, yet was not yet returning worthy in works, through the Word incarnate he anticipates the son's return. He came to the earth before that man came to the house of confession, not deserting the Word itself, but in the Word condescending as far as our pilgrimage. The arm, that is, the Son, he humbled into the embrace of the one returning. The father fell upon the son's neck, when he laid upon him the light yoke of his own love, and kissed him, that there might be shown what is said elsewhere: Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth (Cant. I). Returning, 313Bhe receives the kiss of charity from the father, when through grace he is made certain of pardon.

VERS. 22.-- Bring forth quickly, etc. The first robe, the garment of innocence, in which the first man was founded, but after the fault he knew himself naked, and took a garment of skin, that is, a mortal one. A ring. That is, the seal of faith, with which the promises are sealed in the hearts of believers. In the hand. That is, in working: that through works faith may shine out, and through faith works may be made firm. And shoes. That is, fortify the examples of those before you in the office of preaching, that both the work may be adorned by living well, and the course by preparing for things eternal.

VERS. 23.-- Bring the calf. Preach Christ and make known his death, that a man may both believe in his heart, 313Cby imitating him who was slain, and receive with his mouth the sacrament of the passion unto amendment.

VERS. 24.-- And they began to feast. Not only is the son refreshed for whom the calf was slain, but the father also and his servants, because the refreshment of God and of the saints is the salvation of sinners. And note that after the robe, the ring, the shoe were given, the calf is sacrificed, because unless a man has put on the hope of the first immortality, unless he has fortified his works with the ring of faith, unless he has preached that very faith by confessing it, he cannot take part in the heavenly sacraments.

VERS. 25.-- Now his son was, etc. The son, that is, the people of Israel, who had not gone far off to the worship of idols. Neither was he in the house, because he did not penetrate the inward things of the law: in the literal sense, content, working earthly things, 313Dawaiting earthly things. This is he who bought a field, and five yoke of oxen, who, pressed by the burden of the law, enjoys earthly senses, and took a wife in the affection of the flesh. And when he came. The son draws near to the house when someone among that people, understanding the law spiritually, condemns the labours of servile work, and considers the liberty of the Church. He hears music and a choir, that is, men full of the Spirit preaching the Gospel with concordant voices. He calls one of the servants, when he takes up some one of the prophets to read. And he questions him, while he searches in him what these feasts of the Church may be in which he does not see himself to be.

VERS. 26.-- He asked. And now Israel asks why God rejoices in the taking up of the Gentiles, and by reason of 314Aenvy he does not know the father's will, and stands outside, although he is entreated through heralds.

VERS. 28.-- Being indignant. The Jews were indignant and complained, because Jesus feasted with the Gentiles. And still they are indignant at the salvation of the Gentiles and will not go in. But when the fulness of the Gentiles has come in, the father will go out to entreat Israel, when the calling of the Jews shall be made manifest.

VERS. 29.-- Behold, so many years. (HIER.) Following the righteousness of the law, he is not subject to the righteousness of God. For what greater righteousness of God is there than to pardon the penitent?

Thy commandment, etc. As though it were not to transgress it, to envy another's salvation, or to boast oneself of one's righteousness.

VERS. 30.-- Thou hast killed for him the calf. They confess that 314BChrist has come, but envy wills not to be saved. So it is of the Jew and the Gentile. But generally it can be taken of the just man and the sinner. He hath devoured his substance, etc. The harlots are the superstitions of the Gentiles, with whom he squanders his substance who, having left the wedlock of the Word of God, fornicates with the demon in foul greed. Son, thou art always, etc. He does not refute him as one lying, but approving his perseverance with himself, and inviting him to the full enjoyment of a mightier exultation. All that is mine, etc. In eternal blessedness, both the single things of all and all the things of each single one.

CAPUT XVI.

VERS. 1.-- Ad discipulos. Quia Pharisaei superbi et avari sicut petentibus veniam, ita egentibus pecuniam 314Cnegabant; ideo Salvator nunc ad illos, nunc illis audientibus, ad discipulos de pietate verba facit. Homo quidam. Villicus proprie, villae custos, sed ponitur hic pro oeconomo, id est dispensatore, qui universam substantiam domus dispensat. In hoc quippe dispensatore non omnia ad imitandum debemus sumere, scilicet ut fraudulenter erga Deum agamus. Sed econtrario istae similitudines dantur, ut intelligamus si laudari potuit a Domino qui fraudem faciebat, quanto magis placet Deo qui secundum ejus praeceptum opera illa facit.

VERS. 6.-- Centum cados. Cadus Graece, amphora dicitur Latine, continens tres urnas. Chorus modiis triginta impletur. Simpliciter sic potest accipi; quisquis 314Dindigentiam pauperis, vel ex dimidia, vel ex quinta parte alleviat misericordiae suae mercede donandus est.

VERS. 8.-- Et laudavit Dominus. Si dispensator iniqui mammonae Domini voce laudatur quod de re iniqua sibi justitiam praeparavit, et passus dispendium, tamen laudat prudentiam ejus, non quod adversum Dominum fraudulenter egerit, sed quod pro se prudenter fecerit: quanto magis Christus qui nullum damnum sustinere potest, et pronus est ad clementiam, laudabit discipulos, si in sibi creditos misericordes fuerint. Filii hujus saeculi. Cujus quisque opera agit, ejus et filius dicitur; unde: Filii hujus saeculi, id est tenebrarum; et filii lucis, aeternae vitae.

315AVERS. 9.-- Facite vobis. Mammona lingua Syrorum divitiae iniquitatis, quia de iniquitate collectae sunt. Si ergo iniquitas bene dispensata vertitur in justitiam, quanto magis epulae divini sermonis, in quo nulla est iniquitas, bonum dispensatorem levant in coelum.

VERS. 10.-- Qui fidelis. In pecunia cum paupere participanda. Et in majori. Adhaerens Creatori, et cum eo spiritus unus effectus. Sed qui temporalia quae possidet non bene dispensat, aeternorum sibi gloriam de qua tumet evacuat.

VERS. 11.-- Si ergo, etc. Qui non dat fratribus ad utendum quod a Deo pro omnibus est creatum, iniquus erit in spirituali pecunia dividenda, ut non pro necessitate, sed pro personis doctrinam Domini 315Bdividat.

VERS. 12.-- Et si in alieno. Si carnales divitias quae labuntur non bene dispensatis, veras et aeternas divitias quis dabit vobis?

VERS. 13.-- Nemo servus. Corripiens avaritiam dicit amatores pecuniae Deum amore non posse; qui ergo Deum amat pecuniam contemnit: non valet enim simul transitoria et aeterna diligere.

Aut uni adhaerebit. (AUG.) Non quidem diliget. Nemo enim diligit, etc., usque ad et de bonitate ejus et impunitate sibi blanditur.

Non potestis, etc. Non ait habere divitias, sed servire. Servit qui custodit ut servus; sed qui servitutis excussit jugum, distribuit ut Dominus. Qui servit mammonae, illi utique servit qui merito suae perversitatis 315Crebus istis praepositus princeps mundi a Domino dicitur.

VERS. 14.-- Audiebant autem. Monebat Dominus scribas et Pharisaeos non superbe sapere, non de sua justitia praesumere, sed peccatores poenitentes suscipere: et sua quae forte inciderent peccata, eleemosynis redimere. Sed illi praeceptorem misericordiae, humilitatis, et parcimoniae, derident, quasi minus utilia et nunquam facienda noxius imperaret, vel certe utilia, sed a se jam perfecta superfluus ingereret.

VERS. 15.-- Et ait, etc. Dum peccatores quasi infirmos et desperatores contemnitis, et vos perfectos in omnibus cunctaeque fragilitatis expertes, eleemosynarum remedio non egere creditis. Sed haec tumoris 315Daltitudo quam juste damnanda sit, videt ille qui illuminat abscondita tenebrarum, et manifestat consilia cordium.

VERS. 16.-- Lex et prophetae. Disputantem contra philargyriam deridebant, quasi contrarium legi et prophetis: cum in lege multi divites Deo placuerunt, et cum lex suis observatoribus bona terrae promittat, quibus occurrit docens distantiam esse inter legem et Evangelium: quia in lege carnalibus propter terram praecipiuntur minora, et in Evangelio spiritualibus propter coelum praecipiuntur majora.

Et omnis. Magna vis est cum de terra genitu coelum quaerit possidere per virtutem, quod non potuit tenere per naturam, cum linguas etiam deridentium 316Acontemnit, sicut Dominus irritus est a Pharisaeis. Omnis qui vim facit, vehementi studio properat, non torpenti lentescit affectu. Est ergo fidei religiosa violentia, segnities criminosa. Faciamus ergo vim naturae, ut non ad terrena demergat, sed ad superna se erigat.

VERS. 17.-- Facilius est. Quamvis dicam, Lex et prophetae usque ad Joannem, tamen non praedico legis destructionem: quia facilius est maxima mundi elementa quam minima legis praecepta transire, quia etiam illa quae levia vel superstitiosa videntur, non vacant a sacramentis spiritualibus quando omnia et in Evangelio recapitulatur.

VERS. 18.-- Omnis etc. (AMBR.) Non omne conjugium est a Deo. Non enim gentilibus Christianae 316Bcopulantur, cum lex prohibeat. Ubi est impar conjugium, lex Dei non est.

VERS. 19.-- Homo quidam. Contra derisores avaros quod proposuit exemplo astruit, scilicet quod dives apud inferos torquetur, qui Lazarum non fecit sibi amicum, a quo posset recipi in aeterna tabernacula, et magis videtur narratio quam parabola.

Qui induebatur purpura, etc. (GREG.) Purpura color regii habitus est, conchis marinis, etc., usque ad quia viscera pietatis ignoravit, quia praecepta pretio redimere noluit.

VERS. 20.-- Et erat quidam. Si qua reprehensibilia in pauperibus videmus, despicere non debemus, quia fortasse quos morum infirmitas vulnerat, medicina paupertatis curat. Ulceribus plenus. Hujus 316Culcera dives fastidiosus exhorret, ne inter pretiosas epulas, unguentatosque convivas, fetorem ulcerum lambentibus canibus sustineat.

VERS. 21.-- Cupiens. In una re duo judicia Dei explentur: ex visione pauperis diviti non miserenti cumulus damnationis infertur; et rursum ex visione divitis, tentatus quotidie pauper probatur, quem ad majorem ipsius probationem paupertas simul et aegritudo, et visa divitis copia, et nulla sibi adhibita consolatio, afficiunt. Canes. Potest in lingua canum lingua significari adulantium qui vulnera lingunt, dum mala quae in nobis reprehendimus improbo favore laudant.

VERS. 22.-- In sinum Abrahae. Sinus Abrahae est requies beatorum pauperum, quorum est regnum 316Dcoelorum, in quo post hanc vitam recipiuntur. Sepultura inferni poenarum profunditas est, quae superbos et immisericordes post hanc vitam vorat. Et sepultus est. In lege non tenacia, sed rapina damnatur; in Evangelio dives quod sua non dedit torquetur.

VERS. 23.-- Elevans autem. A longe vident infideles, dum in imo positi ante diem judicii fideles super se in requie attendunt, quorum gaudia post contemplari non possunt. Longe est quod conspiciunt, quia illuc per meritum non attingunt.

Vidit Abraham. (GREG.) Credendum est, quod ante retributionem extremi judicii injusti in requie, etc., usque ad et de illorum poena torquentur quos inutiliter amaverunt.

317AVERS. 24.-- Ut intingat. Qui micas cadentes de mensa Lazaro non dedit, de extremo ejus digito aquam distillari in ore suo cupit, et qui minima in vita non dedit, in inferno minima quaerit.

VERS. 25.-- Quia recepisti. (AUG.) Nota quod dives iste aliquid boni habuit, ex quo bonum transitorium in hac vita recepit, et Lazarus aliquid mali habuit, sed ignis inopia purgavit.

VERS. 26.-- Et in his omnibus. Sicut reprobi a poenis ad gloriam sanctorum transire volunt, ita justi per misericordiam mente ire volunt ad positos in tormentis, ut eos liberent; sed non possunt, quia justorum animae etsi in naturae suae bonitate misericordiam habent, jam tamen auctoris sui justitiae conjunctae, tanta rectitudine constringuntur, ut nulla 317Bad reprobos compassione moveantur.

VERS. 27.-- Rogo ergo. Postquam ardenti de se spes tollitur, animus ad propinquos recurrit, quia reproborum mentem poena sua quandoque inutiliter erudit ad charitatem, ut jam tunc etiam suos spiritualiter diligant, qui hic dum peccata diligerent, nec se amabant. Servatur ergo diviti ad poenam, et cognitio pauperis quem despexit, et memoria fratrum quos reliquit, ut de visa despecti gloria, et de poena inutiliter amatorum amplius torqueatur. Ut mittas. Serius dives iste magister incipit esse, cum jam nec discendi tempus habeat, nec docendi.

VERS. 31.-- Si Moysen. Qui verba legis despiciunt, Redemptoris praecepta qui ex mortuis surrexit, quanto subtiliora sunt, tanto haec difficilius implebunt, 317Cet cujus dicta implere renuunt, ei procul dubio credere recusant.

CHAPTER XVI.

VERS. 1.-- To the disciples. Because the Pharisees, being proud and avaricious, just as they denied pardon to those who asked it, so 314Cdenied money to those in need; therefore the Saviour now speaks to them, now in their hearing speaks to the disciples, concerning mercy. A certain man. A bailiff, properly, is the keeper of a farm, but the word is put here for a steward, that is, a dispenser, who dispenses the whole substance of the house. For in this dispenser we ought not to take all things for imitation, namely that we should act fraudulently toward God. But on the contrary these similitudes are given, that we may understand that if he who wrought fraud could be praised by his lord, how much more does he please God who does those works according to his precept.

VERS. 6.-- A hundred cadi. A cadus in Greek is called in Latin an amphora, holding three urns. A chorus is filled with thirty pecks. It can simply be taken thus: whoever 314Drelieves the need of a poor man, whether by a half or by a fifth part, is to be endowed with the wages of his own mercy.

VERS. 8.-- And the Lord commended. If the dispenser of the unjust mammon is praised by the Lord's own voice because out of an unjust thing he made ready righteousness for himself, and, though he suffered loss, yet he praises his prudence, not because he had acted fraudulently against his lord, but because he had acted prudently for himself: how much more shall Christ, who can sustain no loss and is inclined to clemency, praise the disciples, if they have been merciful toward those entrusted to them. The children of this world. Whose works a man does, his son also he is called; whence: The children of this world, that is, of darkness; and the children of light, of eternal life.

315AVERS. 9.-- Make unto you. Mammon, in the tongue of the Syrians, is the riches of iniquity, because they were gathered out of iniquity. If therefore iniquity well dispensed is turned into righteousness, how much more do the feasts of the divine word, in which there is no iniquity, lift up a good dispenser into heaven.

VERS. 10.-- He that is faithful. In money that is to be shared with the poor man. And in that which is greater. Cleaving to the Creator, and made one spirit with him. But he who does not well dispense the temporal things he possesses, empties out for himself the glory of eternal things, over which he swells.

VERS. 11.-- If therefore, etc. He who does not give to his brethren for their use what was created by God for all, will be unjust in dividing spiritual money, so that he divides the Lord's 315Bteaching not according to need, but according to persons.

VERS. 12.-- And if in that which is another's. If you do not well dispense the carnal riches which slip away, who will give you the true and eternal riches?

VERS. 13.-- No servant. Rebuking avarice, he says that the lovers of money are not able God with love; he therefore who loves God despises money: for it avails not to love things transitory and things eternal at once.

Or he will hold to the one. (AUG.) He will not indeed love. For no man loves, etc., as far as and he flatters himself concerning his goodness and his impunity.

You cannot, etc. He does not say to have riches, but to serve them. He serves who guards them as a servant; but he who has shaken off the yoke of servitude distributes as a lord. He who serves mammon assuredly serves him who, being set over these things by the desert of his own perversity, 315Cis called by the Lord the prince of the world.

VERS. 14.-- Now they heard. The Lord was admonishing the scribes and Pharisees not to be proudly wise, not to presume upon their own righteousness, but to receive penitent sinners: and to redeem by alms their own sins into which they might chance to fall. But they deride the teacher of mercy, of humility and of thrift, as though, being harmful, he were commanding things less useful and never to be done, or else things useful indeed, but, being superfluous, were pressing upon them things already accomplished by themselves.

VERS. 15.-- And he said, etc. While you despise sinners as weak and as men without hope, and believe yourselves perfect in all things and free of every frailty, and to have no need of the remedy of almsgiving. But how justly this height of swelling 315Dis to be condemned, he sees who enlightens the hidden things of darkness, and makes manifest the counsels of hearts.

VERS. 16.-- The law and the prophets. They derided him as he disputed against the love of money, as though it were contrary to the law and the prophets: since in the law many rich men pleased God, and since the law promises to those who keep it the good things of the earth; whom he meets, teaching that there is a distance between the law and the Gospel: because in the law lesser things are commanded to carnal men for the sake of the earth, and in the Gospel greater things are commanded to spiritual men for the sake of heaven.

And every one. Great is the violence, when by birth from earth he seeks to possess by virtue heaven, which he could not hold by nature, when he despises even 316Athe tongues of those who deride, as the Lord is made void by the Pharisees. Every one who does violence hastens with vehement zeal, he does not slacken with a torpid affection. There is therefore a religious violence of faith, and a criminal sloth. Let us then do violence to nature, that it may not sink us to earthly things, but raise itself to things above.

VERS. 17.-- It is easier. Although I say, The law and the prophets until John, yet I do not preach the destruction of the law: because it is easier for the greatest elements of the world to pass than the least precepts of the law, because even those which seem light or superstitious are not empty of spiritual sacraments, since all things are recapitulated in the Gospel also.

VERS. 18.-- Every one etc. (AMBR.) Not every wedlock is from God. For Christian women are not 316Bjoined to Gentiles, since the law forbids it. Where there is an unequal wedlock, the law of God is not there.

VERS. 19.-- A certain man. Against the avaricious mockers he builds up by an example what he had set forth, namely that the rich man is tormented among those below, who did not make Lazarus his friend, by whom he might have been received into eternal tabernacles; and it seems rather a narrative than a parable.

Who was clothed in purple, etc. (GREG.) Purple is the colour of royal apparel, from sea-shells, etc., as far as because he knew nothing of the bowels of pity, because he was unwilling to redeem the precepts with a price.

VERS. 20.-- And there was a certain. If we see anything blameworthy in the poor, we ought not to despise them, because perhaps those whom weakness of manners wounds, the medicine of poverty heals. Full of sores. At this man's 316Csores the fastidious rich man shudders, lest amid precious banquets and perfumed guests he should endure the stench of sores with the dogs licking them.

VERS. 21.-- Desiring. In one thing two judgments of God are fulfilled: from the sight of the poor man a heap of damnation is brought upon the rich man who does not pity; and again from the sight of the rich man the poor man, tempted daily, is proved, whom, for his own greater proving, poverty together with sickness, and the beheld plenty of the rich man, and no consolation applied to him, afflict. The dogs. In the tongue of the dogs there can be signified the tongue of flatterers who lick wounds, while with wicked favour they praise the evils which we blame in ourselves.

VERS. 22.-- Into Abraham's bosom. Abraham's bosom is the rest of the blessed poor, whose is the kingdom 316Dof heaven, into which after this life they are received. The burial of hell is the depth of punishments, which devours the proud and the unmerciful after this life. And he was buried. In the law not niggardliness but rapine is condemned; in the Gospel the rich man is tormented because he did not give what was his own.

VERS. 23.-- And lifting up. The unfaithful see afar off, while, set in the depth before the day of judgment, they mark the faithful above them in rest, whose joys afterwards they cannot behold. What they descry is far off, because they do not reach thither by merit.

He saw Abraham. (GREG.) It is to be believed that before the retribution of the last judgment the unjust in rest, etc., as far as and they are tormented by the punishment of those whom they loved uselessly.

317AVERS. 24.-- That he may dip. He who did not give to Lazarus the crumbs falling from the table, desires that water be distilled into his mouth from the tip of that man's finger; and he who gave not the least things in life, in hell seeks the least things.

VERS. 25.-- Because thou hast received. (AUG.) Note that this rich man had something of good, out of which he received a transitory good in this life, and Lazarus had something of evil, but the want of fire purged it.

VERS. 26.-- And in all these things. Just as the reprobate wish to pass over from their punishments to the glory of the saints, so the just wish in mind through mercy to go to those set in torments, that they may free them; but they cannot, because the souls of the just, though in the goodness of their own nature they have mercy, are yet now joined to the righteousness of their Author and are bound with so great a rectitude that they are moved by no 317Bcompassion toward the reprobate.

VERS. 27.-- I beseech thee therefore. After hope for himself is taken from the burning man, his mind runs back to his kinsmen, because the punishment of the reprobate sometimes uselessly instructs their mind unto charity, so that then at last they love even their own spiritually, who here, while they loved their sins, did not even love themselves. There is kept therefore for the rich man unto punishment both the knowledge of the poor man whom he despised, and the memory of the brethren whom he left behind, that he may be the more tormented by the beheld glory of the despised and by the punishment of those he uselessly loved. That thou wouldst send. Too late does this rich man begin to be a master, when he has now neither time for learning nor for teaching.

VERS. 31.-- If Moses. They who despise the words of the law, by how much the more subtle the precepts of the Redeemer, who rose from the dead, are, by so much the more hardly will they fulfil these, 317Cand him whose sayings they refuse to fulfil, him without doubt they refuse to believe.

CAPUT XVII.

VERS. 1.-- Et ait ad discipulos. Sciendum cum Dominus de misericordia loqueretur, Pharisaei avari in irrisione perseverabant, in quo infirmis auditoribus scandalum, ne attenderent, faciebant. Hos ergo increpat loquens apostolis, scilicet quod illi suo vitio faciebant, ut evenirent scandala per se quae non poterant non evenire in mundo aerumnis et erroribus plenissimo.

VERS. 2.-- Si lapis molaris, etc. Secundum ritum provinciae ubi erat ista criminum poena, ut alligato saxo in profundum demergerentur. Et vere utilius 317Dinnoxium poena gravissima vitam finire, quam laedendo fratrem mortem moereri perpetuam. De pusillis. Non aetate, sed fide et animo, qui occasiones quaerunt quomodo scandalizentur, ideo consulere oportet vos his ne occasione vestri recedant a fide et a salute.

VERS. 3.-- Attendite vobis. Quo ordine, scandala declinare et vae perpetuum evitare possimus, insinuat. Sed si nos attendimus ne quem laedamus, si poenitentem zelo justitiae corripimus, si ex corde poenitenti dimittimus, nec jubemur passim peccanti, sed poenitentiam agenti dimittere. Primum ergo est peccantem increpare, ut postea poenitenti possimus dimittere. Ita enim peccat qui peccantem videns 318Atacet, sicut qui poenitenti non indulget. Et si poenitentiam. Aliter fratri poenitenti, aliter inimico persequenti veniam dare praecipimur. Fratri concessa remissione socia charitate communicamus, persecutori malum nobis volenti, vel si potest facienti, bona volumus et facimus quod possumus.

VERS. 4.-- Septies. Solet universitas septenario designari. Praecipitur ergo ut omnia peccata dimittantur, vel semper poenitenti dimittatur.

VERS. 5.-- Adauge. Cum supra dixisset (Luc. XVI): Qui fidelis est in minimo, etc. Apostoli qui in alieno et in minimo, id est in terrenorum contemptu fuerant fideles, in suo et majori fidem postulant augeri, ut qui ex fide jam habent exordia virtutis, in eadem proficiant, donec ad perfectionem veniant.

318BVERS. 6.-- Arbori moro. Per morum colore sanguineo cujus fructus et virgulta rubent, Evangelium crucis exprimitur, quae per fidem apostolorum de gente Judaeorum (in qua velut in stirpe generis tenebatur verbum praedicationis) est eradicata et in mare gentium transplantata, cui etiam convenit subjecta parabola de ministris verbi. Folia mori serpenti subjecta necem inferunt, et verbum crucis ut cuncta salutaria confert, cuncta noxia tollit. Fructus mori in flore albet, formatus irrutilat, maturitate nigrescit: sic diabolus, flore angelicae naturae et potestate rutilantis praevaricando terror inhorruit odore, qui per fidem excluditur.

VERS. 7.-- Quis autem, etc. Servus arans aut pascens doctor est Ecclesiae, de quo dicitur: Nemo 318Cmittens manum ad aratrum et aspiciens retro, aptus est regno (Luc. IX) Dei. Et Dominus Petro dicit: Pasce oves meas (Joan. XXI). Qui servus de agro regreditur, cum intermisso opere praedicandi, quasi ad curiam conscientiae rediens sua dicta vel facta pertractat, cui Dominus non statim jubet ab hac vita transire, et aeterna quiete refoveri, sed domi parare quod coenet, id est post laborem apertae locutionis, humilitatem propriae conversationis exhibere, in tali enim conscientia Deus coenat.

VERS. 8.-- Et ministra mihi. Ministrat conditori qui ejus naturam considerans, et ejus judicia pertimescens, de suis se virtutibus humiliat. Minister enim a minori statu dicitur.

VERS. 10.-- Sic et vos. Postquam ad perfectionem 318Dfidei exhortatus est, per quam fiunt mirabilia opera, etiam contra rerum naturam monet, ne quis de suis operibus glorietur, sed quanto fides est foris virtutibus excellentior, tanto conscientia intus sit humilior, quia Domino jure debemus obsequium. Servi inutiles. Si homo ab homine servo non uniforme, sed multiplex ministerium exigit, nec gratiam habet, quanto magis vos qui sine me nihil potestis, non unius usum operis vel laboris exercere debetis, sed semper novis studiis augere priora, nec vos jactetis, quia bene servitis.

Quod debuimus facere. Qui venit ministrare non ministrari, nos debitores sibi fecit, ne nostris operibus confidentes, sed ejus examen paventes, omnibus 319Aimpletis quae praecepta sunt, nos imperfectos esse recognoscamus, quandiu peregrinamur a Deo.

VERS. 11.-- Et factum est. Post parabolam qua invitat ad humanitatem et perseverantiam in diversis officiis Dei, reprehenduntur ingrati de impensis beneficiis.

VERS. 12.-- Occurrerunt, etc. Leprosi sunt haeretici, qui quasi varios colores habentes in eodem corpore, varias sectas, nunc falsitatis, nunc veritatis, permiscent in eadem praedicatione. Hi autem quia vitantur et ab Ecclesia removentur longe, necesse habent ut magno clamore interpellent.

Qui steterunt, etc. Qui contra Decalogum peccaverunt, nec amando Deum (de quo male sentiunt), nec proximum, a quo dividuntur, sub denario ad Deum 319Bclamant et sanantur, dum in Ecclesiae societate doctrinam integram veramque assequuntur, et omnia secundum Catholicae fidei regulam disserunt, et varietate mendaciorum quasi lepra carent. Sed unus gratias agit, id est qui in unitate Ecclesiae per humilitatem remanent; qui vero per superbiam elati mundatori sunt ingrati, novem sunt, quia per unitatem a perfectione denarii deficiunt.

VERS. 13.-- Et levaverunt vocem. Quia in verbis Salvatoris se errasse cognoscunt, salvandi praeceptorem vocant, et per cognitionem praeceptoris redeunt ad formam salutis.

VERS. 14.-- Ite, etc. Quisquis haeretica pravitate, vel gentili superstitione, vel Judaica perfidia, vel fraterno schismate per Dei gratiam mundatus caruerit, 319Cnecesse est ut ad Ecclesiam veniat, coloremque fidei verum aliis similem ostendat.

VERS. 15.-- Unus autem. Unus autem regressus significat unius Ecclesiae devotam in Christo humilitatem, qui cadens ad pedes ejus gratias agens, quia repressis praesumptionis cogitationibus, quam sit infirmus videt, nihil virtutis sibi tribuit; bona quae agit de misericordia intelligit.

VERS. 16.-- Et cecidit in faciem. Cadit in faciem, qui de perpetratis malis erubescit. Ibi enim cadit homo, ubi confunditur. Qui in faciem cadit, videt quo cadat; qui retro cadit, non videt. Boni ergo in faciem cadunt, quia humiliant se in his visibilibus, ut ad invisibilia erigantur; mali retro cadunt, quia cadunt in invisibilibus, ubi non vident quid eos sequatur.

319DVERS. 19.-- Surge. Qui infirmitatem suam agnoscens humiliter ante Deum jacuit, divina consolatione surgere ad fortiora opera, et ire crescendo usque ad perfectiora jubetur. Fides tua. Fides quae ad agendas gratias inclinavit, econtra perfidia illos damnat qui de acceptis beneficiis sunt ingrati. Te salvum fecit. In superiori parabola decernitur fidem per humilitatem debere augeri. In ista rebus ipsis manifeste ostenditur, non fidei tantum agnitam rationem, sed fidei exsecutam operationem esse, quae salvum faciat credentem.

VERS. 20.-- Interrogatus. Ideo interrogant, quia, sicut Lucas inferius aperuit, existimabant quod, 320Aveniente Domino Hierosolymam, confestim regnum ejus manifestaretur.

Non venit regnum Dei, etc. Quia, quando veniet, neque ab angelis, neque ab hominibus potest observari, sicut Dominicae incarnationis tempus, et prophetarum vaticiniis praefixum, et angelorum est praeconiis manifestatum, adeo ut conceptus est, natus, baptizatus, praedicans, moriens, resurgens, ad coelos ascendens, comitantibus indesinenter, vel angelorum, vel hominum, vel certe miraculorum sit declaratus indiciis.

VERS. 21.-- Ecce enim regnum. Non poterit observari, quia regnum meum non est corporale, ut putatis, sed spirituale, quod fide jam incepit.

Regnum Dei. Vel sic potest accipi responsio Domini: 320BRegnum Dei intra vos est, qui quandoque venturus Judex omnium, nunc etiam regnat in cordibus fidelium. Sed cum haeretici turbant Ecclesiam pravis dogmatibus, desiderant fideles ut Dominus, una die ad terras rediens, si fieri posset, veritatem fidei intimaret. Sed non videbunt, quia quod semel Evangelico fulgore est exhibitum, non est opus corporali visione Domini firmari.

VERS. 22.-- Venient dies. Noluit dicere quando veniret, sed venturum dixit, ut incuteret omnibus terrorem imminentis judicii, nec securitatem afferret dilationis.

Desideretis. Post meam ascensionem, quando vos in hoc exsilio mundi ingemiscentes desiderabitis me videre in gloria mea, quae voluntas laudanda, non 320Ctamen in hoc mundo implenda. Unum diem. Bene unum dicit, quia in illa gloria, de qua propheta ait: Melior est dies una in atriis tuis super millia (Psal. LXXXIII), nulla est tenebrarum interruptio, nulla saltem memoria miseriae vel doloris. Hujus diei praesentiam bonum est desiderare, non tamen magnitudine desiderii nobis fingere somnia, quasi instet dies Domini, quod ab aliquibus faciendum praedicit.

VERS. 23.-- Et dicent, etc. Fuere quidam qui computantes curricula aetatum, consummationis saeculi, certum annum, diem et horam se dicerent invenisse.

VERS. 24.-- Nam sicut, etc. Nolite illis credere, quia secundus adventus repentinus erit, et ita manifestus, quod nullus poterit ignorare de sub coelo, 320Dquia discrimen judicii sub coelo, hoc est, in aeris medio geritur.

VERS. 25.-- Primum autem, etc. Loquens de gloria adventus, inserit etiam de passione, ut cum morientem viderent quem regnaturum audierant, dolorem passionis, spe promissae claritatis mitigarent.

VERS. 26.-- Et sicut factum est. Subitum adventus sui diem pluribus astruit exemplis, et quem fulguri (cito omnia transvolanti) comparaverat, diebus Noe, vel Loth aequiparat, quando repentinus mortalibus supervenit interitus.

VERS. 27.-- Edebant, etc. Non connubia vel alimenta damnantur, cum in his successionis, in illis naturae posita sint subsidia, sed immoderatus licitorum 321Ausus arguitur. Quia enim his se totos dando, Dei judicia contemnebant, igne vel aqua perierunt. Usque in diem. Allegorice: Noe arcam aedificat, cum Dominus fideres in Ecclesia congregat. Quam et consummatam ingreditur cum hanc in die judicii praesentia sua illustrat. Sed dum arca aedificatur, iniqui luxuriantur, et dum intratur, aeterna damnatione plectuntur.

VERS. 28.-- Similiter, etc. Praetermisso maximo Sodomitarum scelere, sola ea commemorat quae vel levia, vel nulla esse putarentur, ut intelligas quali poena feriantur illicita, si licita et ea sine quibus vita non ducitur, immoderatius acta, igne et sulphure punientur.

Loth. Allegorice: Loth, qui interpretatur declinans, est populus electorum, qui dum in Sodomis, 321Bid est inter reprobos ut advena moratur, quantum valet scelera eorum declinat. Exeunte Loth, Sodoma perit, quia in consummatione saeculi exibunt angeli, et separabunt malos de medio justorum, et mittent eos in caminum ignis (Matth. XIII).

VERS. 30.-- Filius hominis. Qui interim apparens omnia videt, tunc apparens omnia judicabit, quando cunctos judiciorum suorum oblitos huic saeculo conspexerit mancipatos, ut merito debeant cum ipso quem inhabitant orbe deleri.

VERS. 31.-- In illa die. Hucusque de hoc quod regnum, id est Christus, non cum observatione, sed insperatus adveniat: nunc a qualibus ipse adventus exspectari debeat. In tecto est, qui carnalia excedens velut in aura libera spiritualiter vivit. Vasa ejus 321Cin domo, sunt concupiscentiae carnales, quas prius amaverat, sed superna petens in mundo reliquerat, quas imminente judicio necesse est ne repetat.

Et qui in agro. Quod dixerat: Eum qui in agro est retro non debere redire, ne de his tantum dictum videretur, qui aperte de agro redituri sunt, id est Dominum palam negaturi; ostendit multos qui cum faciem in anteriora videntur tenere, animo tamen retro respiciunt.

VERS. 32.-- Memores estote. Uxor Loth significat illos qui in tribulatione retro respiciunt, et se a spe divinae promissionis avertunt, et ideo statua salis facta est, quia admonendo homines ne similia faciant, corda eorum condit ne sint fatui.

VERS. 33.-- Quicunque. Qui vitam carnalem (negando 321DChristum) in mundo salvare voluerit, in futuro perdet eam; vel qui aeternam salutem animae quaerit, non dubitet eam dare ad mortem hujus temporis.

VERS. 34.-- In illa nocte. Id est in illa tam tenebrosa tribulatione ut in errorem inducantur, si fieri potest, etiam electi (Matth. XXIV). Duo in lecto. Illi scilicet qui otium eligunt, neque saecularibus, neque ecclesiasticis negotiis occupati.

VERS. 35.-- Altera relinquetur. Qui vel humana laude, vel qualibet corruptione statum vitae qua imbutus est, deserit. De quo Jeremias: Viderunt eam hostes, et deriserunt sabbata ejus (Thren. I). Duae erunt, etc. Duae differentiae, sequentium orbem rerum mobilium. Quae femineo genere designantur, 322Aquia majorum consilio reguntur, ut feminae a viris, quae tamen in unum molunt inquantum de suis negotiis Ecclesiae usibus deserviunt. Sed quidam tantum propter aeterna, qui habent tanquam non habentes, quidam propter temporalia, qui in incerto divitiarum sperant (I Tim VI).

VERS. 37.-- Qui dixit illis. Duo quaerentibus unum respondet, sanctos scilicet secum assumendos, aliud subjiciendo innuit, malos scilicet a se repulsos cum diabolo damnandos. Ubicunque, etc. Ubicunque fuerit Dominus corporaliter, illo congregabuntur electi, qui ejus passionem humiliter imitando tanquam de carne ejus saturantur, quorum per resurrectionem renovabitur ut aquilae juventus.

CHAPTER XVII.

VERS. 1.-- And he said to the disciples. It is to be known that when the Lord was speaking of mercy, the avaricious Pharisees persevered in mockery, wherein they made a scandal for weak hearers, that these might not attend. These therefore he rebukes while speaking to the apostles, namely for what they were doing by their own vice, so that scandals came about through them which were not able not to come about in a world most full of hardships and errors.

VERS. 2.-- If a millstone, etc. According to the custom of the province where this punishment of crimes obtained, that with a stone bound to them they should be plunged into the deep. And truly it is more profitable for an innocent man to end his life by the heaviest punishment, than by harming a brother to earn 317Dperpetual death. Of the little ones. Not in age, but in faith and mind, who seek occasions how they may be scandalized; therefore you must take counsel for these, lest by occasion of you they fall away from faith and from salvation.

VERS. 3.-- Take heed to yourselves. He intimates by what order we may turn aside scandals and escape the everlasting woe. But if we take heed that we harm no one, if we rebuke the penitent with the zeal of justice, if from the heart we forgive him who repents, we are not commanded to forgive one who sins at random, but one who does penance. First therefore is it to rebuke him who sins, that afterwards we may be able to forgive him who repents. For he who sees a man sinning and is silent sins as much 318Aas he who does not pardon a penitent. And if he do penance. We are bidden to give pardon in one way to a penitent brother, in another to a persecuting enemy. To a brother we impart fellowship in charity with the remission granted; to a persecutor who wills evil to us, or who does it if he can, we will good things and do what we can.

VERS. 4.-- Seven times. Universality is wont to be designated by the number seven. It is commanded therefore that all sins be forgiven, or that it be always forgiven to one who repents.

VERS. 5.-- Increase. Since he had said above (Luc. XVI): He that is faithful in that which is least, etc. The apostles, who in that which was another's and in that which was least, that is, in the contempt of earthly things, had been faithful, ask that faith be increased in what is their own and greater, that they who from faith already have the beginnings of virtue may advance in the same, until they come to perfection.

318BVERS. 6.-- To the mulberry tree. By the mulberry, of blood-red colour, whose fruit and twigs are ruddy, the Gospel of the cross is expressed, which by the faith of the apostles was uprooted out of the nation of the Jews (in which, as in the stock of the race, the word of preaching was held) and transplanted into the sea of the Gentiles; to which the parable subjoined concerning the ministers of the word also fits. The leaves of the mulberry laid under a serpent bring death, and the word of the cross, as it confers all wholesome things, takes away all noxious ones. The fruit of the mulberry is white in flower, when formed grows red, in ripeness turns black: so the devil, of one shining red with the flower of the angelic nature and with power, by prevaricating bristled up, a terror, in odour, who is shut out through faith.

VERS. 7.-- But which of you, etc. The servant ploughing or feeding is the doctor of the Church, of whom it is said: No man 318Cputting his hand to the plough and looking back is fit for the kingdom (Luc. IX) of God. And the Lord says to Peter: Feed my sheep (Joan. XXI). Which servant returns from the field when, the work of preaching being intermitted, returning as it were to the court of conscience he goes over his own words or deeds; whom the Lord does not at once bid to pass out of this life and be cherished in eternal rest, but to prepare at home what he may sup on, that is, after the labour of open speech, to display the humility of his own manner of life, for in such a conscience God sups.

VERS. 8.-- And minister to me. He ministers to his Maker who, considering his nature and greatly fearing his judgments, humbles himself from his own virtues. For a minister is so called from a lesser estate.

VERS. 10.-- So you also. After he has exhorted to the perfection 318Dof faith, through which wonderful works are done even against the nature of things, he warns that no one should glory of his own works, but that by how much faith is outwardly more excellent in virtues, by so much the conscience within should be more humble, because we owe obedience to the Lord by right. Unprofitable servants. If a man exacts from a man who is his servant not a uniform but a manifold ministry, and gives him no thanks, how much more ought you who without me can do nothing not to practise the use of one work or one labour only, but always to increase former things with new zeal, nor should you boast, because you serve well.

What we ought to do. He who came to minister and not to be ministered unto has made us debtors to himself, that not trusting in our own works, but fearing his examination, when all things 319Aare fulfilled which have been commanded, we may recognize that we are unperfected, so long as we are pilgrims from God.

VERS. 11.-- And it came to pass. After the parable by which he invites to kindness and to perseverance in the diverse offices of God, the ungrateful are reproved concerning benefits bestowed.

VERS. 12.-- There met him, etc. The lepers are heretics, who, having as it were various colours in the same body, mingle various sects, now of falsehood, now of truth, in the same preaching. But because these are shunned and removed far from the Church, they must needs cry out with a great clamour.

Who stood, etc. They who have sinned against the Decalogue, loving neither God (of whom they think ill) nor the neighbour, from whom they are divided, cry under the number ten to God 319Band are healed, while in the fellowship of the Church they attain a whole and true teaching, and expound all things according to the rule of the Catholic faith, and are free of the variety of lies as of leprosy. But one gives thanks, that is, they who remain in the unity of the Church through humility; but they who, puffed up through pride, are ungrateful to him who cleansed them, are nine, because by one unit they fall short of the perfection of the number ten.

VERS. 13.-- And they lifted up their voice. Because they acknowledge that they have erred in the words of the Saviour, they call upon the master who is to save them, and through the knowledge of the master they return to the form of salvation.

VERS. 14.-- Go, etc. Whoever, cleansed by the grace of God, has been rid of heretical depravity, or of Gentile superstition, or of Jewish faithlessness, or of brotherly schism, 319Cmust needs come to the Church, and show a true colour of faith like to that of others.

VERS. 15.-- But one. And the one who returned signifies the devout humility in Christ of the one Church, who, falling at his feet and giving thanks, because with the thoughts of presumption repressed he sees how weak he is, ascribes nothing of virtue to himself; the good things which he does he understands to be of mercy.

VERS. 16.-- And he fell on his face. He falls on his face who blushes for the evils he has committed. For a man falls there where he is put to confusion. He who falls on his face sees whither he falls; he who falls backward does not see. The good therefore fall on their face, because they humble themselves in these visible things, that they may be raised to the invisible; the evil fall backward, because they fall among invisible things, where they do not see what follows them.

319DVERS. 19.-- Arise. He who, acknowledging his own weakness, has lain humbly before God, is bidden by divine consolation to rise to stronger works, and to go on increasing as far as things more perfect. Thy faith. The faith which inclined him to give thanks; on the contrary, faithlessness condemns those who are ungrateful for benefits received. Hath made thee whole. In the parable above it is determined that faith ought to be increased through humility. In this one it is manifestly shown by the facts themselves, that it is not the acknowledged reason of faith only, but the executed working of faith, that makes the believer whole.

VERS. 20.-- Being asked. They ask for this reason, because, as Luke discloses further on, they thought that, when the Lord came 320Ato Jerusalem, his kingdom would forthwith be made manifest.

The kingdom of God cometh not, etc. Because, when it shall come, it can be observed neither by angels nor by men, as the time of the Lord's incarnation was both foreappointed by the oracles of the prophets and made manifest by the proclamations of angels, so that he was declared conceived, born, baptized, preaching, dying, rising, ascending to the heavens, with the tokens either of angels, or of men, or certainly of miracles unceasingly accompanying him.

VERS. 21.-- For behold the kingdom. It will not be able to be observed, because my kingdom is not bodily, as you think, but spiritual, which has already begun by faith.

The kingdom of God. Or the Lord's answer can be taken thus: 320BThe kingdom of God is within you, who, one day to come as the Judge of all, even now reigns in the hearts of the faithful. But when heretics trouble the Church with depraved teachings, the faithful desire that the Lord, returning to the earth for one day, if it could be, would make known the truth of the faith. But they shall not see it, because that which has once been displayed by the brightness of the Gospel has no need to be confirmed by a bodily vision of the Lord.

VERS. 22.-- The days shall come. He would not say when he should come, but he said that he would come, that he might strike into all the terror of the imminent judgment, and not bring the security of a delay.

You shall desire. After my ascension, when you, groaning in this exile of the world, shall desire to see me in my glory, which will is to be praised, yet not 320Cto be fulfilled in this world. One day. Well does he say one, because in that glory, of which the prophet says: Better is one day in thy courts above thousands (Psal. LXXXIII), there is no interruption of darkness, no memory even of misery or of grief. It is good to desire the presence of this day, yet not by the greatness of desire to feign dreams for ourselves, as though the day of the Lord were at hand, which he foretells will be done by some.

VERS. 23.-- And they will say, etc. There have been some who, reckoning the courses of the ages, said that they had found the certain year, day and hour of the consummation of the world.
